========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I suppose I'm here because things have been getting a bit complicated lately. I mean, as a psychologist myself, I know I should probably have a better handle on this, but it’s just so incredibly vivid. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don't mean like a memory or an internal monologue, I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. It happens a lot when I'm out on the trail—I like to hike, it usually clears my head, but lately, it's just him talking to me. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is supposed to be my sanctuary, you know? I'll be mixing colors and suddenly he's right there, commenting on my work or just... speaking. And the thing is, logically, the rational part of my brain—the part that got the bachelor's degree and works with clients—knows he’s dead. I know that. But in the moment? In that exact second when the sound hits my ears? It feels completely, undeniably real. It's like the boundary between what's actually happening and what my mind is producing has just completely dissolved, and I can't figure out how to rebuild it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's... it's really just the auditory. I mean, I've been paying very close attention to that, actually, because, well, given my background, the moment you start experiencing one modality of... when the auditory stuff starts, you immediately start monitoring yourself to see if the visual or tactile is going to follow. It's almost a reflex to self-assess at this point. 

So I’ll be sitting there in my painting room—which, to be fair, has very strong, specific smells anyway, you know, the linseed oil, the turpentine, the various acrylic mediums, so I suppose a subtle olfactory thing might be masked—but no, I haven't smelled anything phantom or out of the ordinary. And out on the trail, it's just the usual pine and damp earth. I definitely haven't seen him, either. Or anything else for that matter. I haven't seen shadows moving in the corner of my eye, I haven't felt a hand on my shoulder or a sudden change in temperature... nothing like that. 

Which, honestly, in a strange way, almost makes the voice more jarring? Because if I actually saw him standing there, maybe my brain could just fully commit to the illusion, you know? But instead, I'm just looking at a completely empty room, smelling my normal oil paints, feeling the wooden handle of the brush in my hand, totally and completely grounded in my physical reality... except for this incredibly clear, undeniable voice cutting right through it. So, no, to answer your question, no visions, no phantom touches, no strange smells. Just the voice.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's... that's a really interesting question, actually, because I've spent a lot of time analyzing the context of when it happens. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. 

But as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.

Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed these experiences happening during times when your mood was significantly elevated, depressed, or mixed, or do they seem to occur independently of your mood state?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch when I barely slept, took on impossible projects, and felt driven to keep going. I had never heard him like that before the mood shift, and it wasn’t something that happened during ordinary grief.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that’s… I mean, that’s the crux of it, isn't it? The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. 

But then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. 

And that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.

I mean, I've been paying very close attention to that, actually, because, well, given my background, the moment you start experiencing one modality of... when the auditory stuff starts, you immediately start monitoring yourself to see if the visual or tactile is going to follow. It's almost a reflex to self-assess at this point. \n\nSo I’ll be sitting there in my painting room—which, to be fair, has very strong, specific smells anyway, you know, the linseed oil, the turpentine, the various acrylic mediums, so I suppose a subtle olfactory thing might be masked—but no, I haven't smelled anything phantom or out of the ordinary. And out on the trail, it's just the usual pine and damp earth. I definitely haven't seen him, either. Or anything else for that matter. I haven't seen shadows moving in the corner of my eye, I haven't felt a hand on my shoulder or a sudden change in temperature... nothing like that. \n\nWhich, honestly, in a strange way, almost makes the voice more jarring? Because if I actually saw him standing there, maybe my brain could just fully commit to the illusion, you know? But instead, I'm just looking at a completely empty room, smelling my normal oil paints, feeling the wooden handle of the brush in my hand, totally and completely grounded in my physical reality... except for this incredibly clear, undeniable voice cutting right through it. So, no, to answer your question, no visions, no phantom touches, no strange smells. Just the voice.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences with Garrett’s voice happening only when you’re alone, and do they ever occur in response to something happening around you, even if you know there’s no one else there?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a really interesting question, actually, because I've spent a lot of time analyzing the context of when it happens. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these experiences happening during times when your mood was significantly elevated, depressed, or mixed, or do they seem to occur independently of your mood state?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s… I mean, that’s the crux of it, isn't it? The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 5\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. 

I felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. 

But the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. 

My sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.

And I don't mean like a memory or an internal monologue, I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. It happens a lot when I'm out on the trail—I like to hike, it usually clears my head, but lately, it's just him talking to me. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is supposed to be my sanctuary, you know? I'll be mixing colors and suddenly he's right there, commenting on my work or just... speaking. And the thing is, logically, the rational part of my brain—the part that got the bachelor's degree and works with clients—knows he’s dead. I know that. But in the moment? In that exact second when the sound hits my ears? It feels completely, undeniably real. It's like the boundary between what's actually happening and what my mind is producing has just completely dissolved, and I can't figure out how to rebuild it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other sensations or perceptions that aren't related to Garrett's voice—like seeing things, feeling physical touches, or smelling odors that others don't notice?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just the auditory. I mean, I've been paying very close attention to that, actually, because, well, given my background, the moment you start experiencing one modality of... when the auditory stuff starts, you immediately start monitoring yourself to see if the visual or tactile is going to follow. It's almost a reflex to self-assess at this point. \n\nSo I’ll be sitting there in my painting room—which, to be fair, has very strong, specific smells anyway, you know, the linseed oil, the turpentine, the various acrylic mediums, so I suppose a subtle olfactory thing might be masked—but no, I haven't smelled anything phantom or out of the ordinary. And out on the trail, it's just the usual pine and damp earth. I definitely haven't seen him, either. Or anything else for that matter. I haven't seen shadows moving in the corner of my eye, I haven't felt a hand on my shoulder or a sudden change in temperature... nothing like that. \n\nWhich, honestly, in a strange way, almost makes the voice more jarring? Because if I actually saw him standing there, maybe my brain could just fully commit to the illusion, you know? But instead, I'm just looking at a completely empty room, smelling my normal oil paints, feeling the wooden handle of the brush in my hand, totally and completely grounded in my physical reality... except for this incredibly clear, undeniable voice cutting right through it. So, no, to answer your question, no visions, no phantom touches, no strange smells. Just the voice.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences with Garrett’s voice happening only when you’re alone, and do they ever occur in response to something happening around you, even if you know there’s no one else there?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a really interesting question, actually, because I've spent a lot of time analyzing the context of when it happens. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these experiences happening during times when your mood was significantly elevated, depressed, or mixed, or do they seem to occur independently of your mood state?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s… I mean, that’s the crux of it, isn't it? The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"The things that usually anchor me—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even sitting with patients—feel flat and unreachable, like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, the crash. Because, you know, when you're running at that kind of voltage, it’s completely unsustainable. Eventually, the system just shorts out. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. 

You asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, "losing interest" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. Take my painting, for example. Just a short time ago, I was tearing up the floors to build the perfect studio, and now? I look at the canvases and I feel absolutely nothing. It’s just dead space. 

And it’s not just the art. Playing my guitar, which is usually my primary way to decompress and center myself... it's just sitting in the corner gathering dust. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. 

But the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.

You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. \n\nBut the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you confirm if the period of elevated mood and energy you described lasted for at least seven consecutive days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Seven days... well, yes, definitely, but it’s... it’s so hard to just surgically extract that one specific week or so and look at it in isolation. Because the reality is that this whole... this entire crisis has been unfolding for about a month now, ever since Garrett died.

You look at a calendar, and a month is just a flip of a page, right? Just four weeks. It feels like I've lived a decade squeezed into thirty days. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.

I mean, as a psychologist myself, I know I should probably have a better handle on this, but it’s just so incredibly vivid. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I don't mean like a memory or an internal monologue, I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. It happens a lot when I'm out on the trail—I like to hike, it usually clears my head, but lately, it's just him talking to me. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is supposed to be my sanctuary, you know? I'll be mixing colors and suddenly he's right there, commenting on my work or just... speaking. And the thing is, logically, the rational part of my brain—the part that got the bachelor's degree and works with clients—knows he’s dead. I know that. But in the moment? In that exact second when the sound hits my ears? It feels completely, undeniably real. It's like the boundary between what's actually happening and what my mind is producing has just completely dissolved, and I can't figure out how to rebuild it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other sensations or perceptions that aren't related to Garrett's voice—like seeing things, feeling physical touches, or smelling odors that others don't notice?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just the auditory. I mean, I've been paying very close attention to that, actually, because, well, given my background, the moment you start experiencing one modality of... when the auditory stuff starts, you immediately start monitoring yourself to see if the visual or tactile is going to follow. It's almost a reflex to self-assess at this point. \n\nSo I’ll be sitting there in my painting room—which, to be fair, has very strong, specific smells anyway, you know, the linseed oil, the turpentine, the various acrylic mediums, so I suppose a subtle olfactory thing might be masked—but no, I haven't smelled anything phantom or out of the ordinary. And out on the trail, it's just the usual pine and damp earth. I definitely haven't seen him, either. Or anything else for that matter. I haven't seen shadows moving in the corner of my eye, I haven't felt a hand on my shoulder or a sudden change in temperature... nothing like that. \n\nWhich, honestly, in a strange way, almost makes the voice more jarring? Because if I actually saw him standing there, maybe my brain could just fully commit to the illusion, you know? But instead, I'm just looking at a completely empty room, smelling my normal oil paints, feeling the wooden handle of the brush in my hand, totally and completely grounded in my physical reality... except for this incredibly clear, undeniable voice cutting right through it. Just the voice.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences with Garrett’s voice happening only when you’re alone, and do they ever occur in response to something happening around you, even if you know there’s no one else there?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a really interesting question, actually, because I've spent a lot of time analyzing the context of when it happens. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these experiences happening during times when your mood was significantly elevated, depressed, or mixed, or do they seem to occur independently of your mood state?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s… I mean, that’s the crux of it, isn't it? I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, the crash. No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.

It's almost a reflex to self-assess at this point. \n\nSo I’ll be sitting there in my painting room—which, to be fair, has very strong, specific smells anyway, you know, the linseed oil, the turpentine, the various acrylic mediums, so I suppose a subtle olfactory thing might be masked—but no, I haven't smelled anything phantom or out of the ordinary. And out on the trail, it's just the usual pine and damp earth. I definitely haven't seen him, either. Or anything else for that matter. I haven't seen shadows moving in the corner of my eye, I haven't felt a hand on my shoulder or a sudden change in temperature... nothing like that. \n\nWhich, honestly, in a strange way, almost makes the voice more jarring? Because if I actually saw him standing there, maybe my brain could just fully commit to the illusion, you know? But instead, I'm just looking at a completely empty room, smelling my normal oil paints, feeling the wooden handle of the brush in my hand, totally and completely grounded in my physical reality... except for this incredibly clear, undeniable voice cutting right through it. So, no, to answer your question, no visions, no phantom touches, no strange smells. Just the voice.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences with Garrett’s voice happening only when you’re alone, and do they ever occur in response to something happening around you, even if you know there’s no one else there?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a really interesting question, actually, because I've spent a lot of time analyzing the context of when it happens. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. It's in those quiet, isolated moments. \n\nBut as for whether it's in response to something happening around me... that's where it gets complicated. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. Sometimes he's commenting on a brushstroke I just made, or sometimes he's just saying my name, or bringing up a memory. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. So it's not a reaction to the environment, it's more like... it's like he's just inserting himself into the environment, completely unprompted, if that makes sense. It's just so incredibly vivid, and I just can't seem to logic my way out of it when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these experiences happening during times when your mood was significantly elevated, depressed, or mixed, or do they seem to occur independently of your mood state?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s… I mean, that’s the crux of it, isn't it? The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, the crash. Because, you know, when you're running at that kind of voltage, it’s completely unsustainable. Eventually, the system just shorts out. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nYou asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, \"losing interest\" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. Take my painting, for example. Just a short time ago, I was tearing up the floors to build the perfect studio, and now? I look at the canvases and I feel absolutely nothing. It’s just dead space. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art. Playing my guitar, which is usually my primary way to decompress and center myself... it's just sitting in the corner gathering dust. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. \n\nBut the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the period of elevated mood and energy you described lasted for at least seven consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Seven days... well, yes, definitely, but it’s... it’s so hard to just surgically extract that one specific week or so and look at it in isolation. Because the reality is that this whole... this entire crisis has been unfolding for about a month now, ever since Garrett died. \n\nIt’s actually staggering to think about it in terms of strict days or weeks, because time just becomes completely distorted when your brain is running at those extremes. You look at a calendar, and a month is just a flip of a page, right? Just four weeks. But for me, in this past month since losing him, I’ve had to navigate the initial, crushing grief, and then that massive, relentless surge of energy that just kept burning and burning day after day—absolutely for longer than seven consecutive days, it just wouldn't stop, it was a sustained state—and now, of course, this total, suffocating emptiness. \n\nIt feels like I've lived a decade squeezed into thirty days. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other periods in your life where you felt similarly unusually energetic, confident, or required much less sleep than usual—periods that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable. But that's just... that's just being an ambitious professional, isn't it? \n\nI've always been someone who throws himself into his work and his hobbies. If I'm doing community theater, I'm fully committed. If I'm playing guitar, I'm practicing until my fingers bleed. But it never felt like... like this. It never felt like a runaway train. It was always directed, purposeful energy that I was in control of. I could turn it off, or at least turn it down, when I needed to sleep or, you know, just be a normal person having dinner with a friend.\n\nSo, no, this specific kind of... this terrifying, uncontrollable voltage, this absolute certainty that I was operating on a higher plane of existence while simultaneously wanting to bite the head off anyone who looked at me sideways... that's entirely new. That's what makes this whole month so incredibly disorienting. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable. But that's just... that's just being an ambitious professional, isn't it? \n\nI've always been someone who throws himself into his work and his hobbies. If I'm doing community theater, I'm fully committed. If I'm playing guitar, I'm practicing until my fingers bleed. But it never felt like... like this. It never felt like a runaway train. It was always directed, purposeful energy that I was in control of. I could turn it off, or at least turn it down, when I needed to sleep or, you know, just be a normal person having dinner with a friend.\n\nSo, no, this specific kind of... this terrifying, uncontrollable voltage, this absolute certainty that I was operating on a higher plane of existence while simultaneously wanting to bite the head off anyone who looked at me sideways... that's entirely new. That's what makes this whole month so incredibly disorienting. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had an episode in your life where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual — a period that lasted for several days or more — even if it was before this recent month?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"At first I felt almost lit up, like I had some urgent purpose no one else could see, but I’d snap into rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we just talked about whether I've ever had anything like this before, and I really, truly don't think I have... but if you're asking about this specific recent episode again, this past month... it's just so hard to articulate the exact texture of it. 

Because, okay, at first... at first it didn't feel like a problem. It felt... well, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this profound, undeniable sensation that I had some urgent, critical purpose that no one else could possibly see or understand. Like I had tapped into some universal current that everyone else was just blindly ignoring. I'd be awake at 3 AM, completely wired, drafting these intricate, sweeping plans for the clinic, or sketching out these massive canvases, and I felt... I felt brilliant. I felt like I was finally operating at my true capacity. 

But the flip side of that—and this is the part that really frightens me now, looking back—is how quickly that light turned into this scorching, toxic heat. If anyone, and I mean *anyone*, questioned what I was doing, or looked at me with even a hint of skepticism, or tried to slow me down... I would just snap. It wasn't just annoyance. It was this absolute, blinding rage. I remember my office manager, Sarah—who is a saint, truly—she just gently suggested that maybe I shouldn't be calling patients on a Sunday morning to discuss their treatment plans. And I absolutely tore into her. I said things that were so cruel, so wildly out of proportion, just because she was standing in the way of this... this momentum I felt I had. It was like I was a speeding car, and anyone who even stepped near the road was going to get obliterated. So, yeah, that elevated feeling, it was there, but it was so tightly coiled with this explosive hostility.

And I don't mean like a memory or an internal monologue, I mean I hear him as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me. It happens a lot when I'm out on the trail—I like to hike, it usually clears my head, but lately, it's just him talking to me. You know, trying to find a trigger or a pattern, because as a psychologist, you want to map the behavior, right? You want to find the antecedent. So, to answer the first part, yes, it's almost exclusively when I'm alone. Like I mentioned, it's usually when I'm out on the trail, which is my solitary time, my time to decompress after seeing clients all week. Or when I'm in my painting room, which is sort of my sanctuary where I just shut the door and immerse myself in the canvas. It doesn't seem to happen when I'm in the middle of a session with a client, or, you know, at the grocery store surrounded by people. Because, as I said before, I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on that trail, or right over my shoulder in my painting room. And it's not like a car backfires and my brain misinterprets it as his voice, if that's what you mean. It's not an illusion based on an external stimulus. It just... happens. And even though that rational, educated part of me knows he’s dead, and knows there is absolutely no one else in the room or on the path with me... in the moment, when I hear it, it feels completely, undeniably real. It's so visceral. I'll actually turn around, fully expecting to see him there, because the acoustic quality of the voice is so perfect. It sounds like it's bouncing off the walls of the room, or cutting through the wind on the trail. The mood piece. Because, you see, when Garrett first passed, I grieved. I grieved heavily, as anyone would. I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. But during all that normal, albeit agonizing, grief, I never heard him. Not once. I’d look at his photos, I’d hold his old jacket, but it was just silence. \n\nBut then… things shifted. And I don’t just mean my mood improved, I mean it was like someone flipped a switch and suddenly the current was running at a thousand volts. I hit this stretch where I was just completely, inexplicably wired. It was about, I don't know, maybe a few weeks ago? I stopped needing sleep. I mean, I’d lie down for maybe an hour, stare at the ceiling, and my brain would just be racing with all these grand ideas. I started taking on these massive, impossible projects. I decided I was going to completely renovate the painting room—stripping the floors, building new easels from scratch—while simultaneously trying to write a book on cognitive behavioral techniques, and taking on extra clients. I just felt this intense, unrelenting drive to keep moving, to keep doing, like I had this boundless energy and if I stopped, I’d explode. \n\nAnd that… that exact period, when everything was dialed up to eleven, that is precisely when the voice started. I only started hearing Garrett’s voice during that wired stretch. I’d be up at 3:00 AM, sanding a floorboard, feeling like I was on top of the world, and suddenly he’d be right there, telling me I missed a spot. It was like the sheer velocity of my brain during that elevated state somehow tore a hole in reality and let his voice through. So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, the crash. Because, you know, when you're running at that kind of voltage, it’s completely unsustainable. Eventually, the system just shorts out. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nYou asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, \"losing interest\" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other periods in your life where you felt similarly unusually energetic, confident, or required much less sleep than usual—periods that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable.
