So, yes, it is absolutely tied to that specific, hyper-elevated mood state. It wasn’t there before, during the ordinary grief, and it didn’t start until I was in that frantic, sleepless overdrive. It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, the crash. Because, you know, when you're running at that kind of voltage, it’s completely unsustainable. Eventually, the system just shorts out. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nYou asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, \"losing interest\" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. Take my painting, for example. Just a short time ago, I was tearing up the floors to build the perfect studio, and now? I look at the canvases and I feel absolutely nothing. It’s just dead space. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art. Playing my guitar, which is usually my primary way to decompress and center myself... it's just sitting in the corner gathering dust. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. \n\nBut the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the period of elevated mood and energy you described lasted for at least seven consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Seven days... well, yes, definitely, but it’s... it’s so hard to just surgically extract that one specific week or so and look at it in isolation. Because the reality is that this whole... this entire crisis has been unfolding for about a month now, ever since Garrett died. \n\nIt’s actually staggering to think about it in terms of strict days or weeks, because time just becomes completely distorted when your brain is running at those extremes. You look at a calendar, and a month is just a flip of a page, right? Just four weeks. But for me, in this past month since losing him, I’ve had to navigate the initial, crushing grief, and then that massive, relentless surge of energy that just kept burning and burning day after day—absolutely for longer than seven consecutive days, it just wouldn't stop, it was a sustained state—and now, of course, this total, suffocating emptiness. \n\nIt feels like I've lived a decade squeezed into thirty days. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other periods in your life where you felt similarly unusually energetic, confident, or required much less sleep than usual—periods that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual — a period that lasted for several days or more — even if it was before this recent month?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about whether I've ever had anything like this before, and I really, truly don't think I have... but if you're asking about this specific recent episode again, this past month... it's just so hard to articulate the exact texture of it. \n\nBecause, okay, at first... at first it didn't feel like a problem. It felt... well, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this profound, undeniable sensation that I had some urgent, critical purpose that no one else could possibly see or understand. If anyone, and I mean *anyone*, questioned what I was doing, or looked at me with even a hint of skepticism, or tried to slow me down... I would just snap. It wasn't just annoyance. It was this absolute, blinding rage. I remember my office manager, Sarah—who is a saint, truly—she just gently suggested that maybe I shouldn't be calling patients on a Sunday morning to discuss their treatment plans. And I absolutely tore into her. I said things that were so cruel, so wildly out of proportion, just because she was standing in the way of this... this momentum I felt I had. Right, I’m sorry, I know I completely derailed your question just now and went off on that tangent about Sarah and the clinic. It’s just... my mind is still constantly picking through the wreckage of what I’ve done over the last few weeks, and it's hard to stay on track. But to give you a definitive, clear answer—no. I really, truly haven't. Before this past month, before losing Garrett, there was never a time in my life where I had an episode like that. 

And I feel like I can say that with a pretty high degree of certainty, especially given my line of work. I mean, being a psychologist, you spend your entire career trained to observe behavior, to track mood fluctuations, to look for these exact kinds of shifts in your clients. I’m hyper-aware of psychological baselines. If I had spent a week in my twenties or thirties sleeping two hours a night, feeling abnormally energized, or acting like I was operating on some elevated plane, I really believe I would have recognized it—or at the very least, one of my colleagues or supervisors would have pulled me aside and pointed it out. 

My baseline for my entire adult life has been almost boringly stable. I’d wake up, go to the office, see my patients, come home... we’d cook dinner, maybe watch a movie, and I’d sleep a solid seven or eight hours. Every single night. I’ve always been very grounded, very predictable. I never had these wild swings or periods where I just didn't need sleep. That’s precisely why this whole experience has been so profoundly terrifying. It’s like a dormant volcano just erupted in the middle of my life and I didn't even know I was living on a fault line. So, looking back at everything before this recent crisis... no. There is absolutely zero history of it. This is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me.

I went through the textbook stages, which, frankly, is a miserable experience when you know exactly what’s happening to you clinically but you can't stop the pain anyway. I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nYou asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, \"losing interest\" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. Take my painting, for example. Just a short time ago, I was tearing up the floors to build the perfect studio, and now? I look at the canvases and I feel absolutely nothing. It’s just dead space. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art. Playing my guitar, which is usually my primary way to decompress and center myself... it's just sitting in the corner gathering dust. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. \n\nBut the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the period of elevated mood and energy you described lasted for at least seven consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Seven days... well, yes, definitely, but it’s... it’s so hard to just surgically extract that one specific week or so and look at it in isolation. Because the reality is that this whole... this entire crisis has been unfolding for about a month now, ever since Garrett died. \n\nIt’s actually staggering to think about it in terms of strict days or weeks, because time just becomes completely distorted when your brain is running at those extremes. You look at a calendar, and a month is just a flip of a page, right? Just four weeks. But for me, in this past month since losing him, I’ve had to navigate the initial, crushing grief, and then that massive, relentless surge of energy that just kept burning and burning day after day—absolutely for longer than seven consecutive days, it just wouldn't stop, it was a sustained state—and now, of course, this total, suffocating emptiness. \n\nIt feels like I've lived a decade squeezed into thirty days. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other periods in your life where you felt similarly unusually energetic, confident, or required much less sleep than usual—periods that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable. But that's just... that's just being an ambitious professional, isn't it? \n\nI've always been someone who throws himself into his work and his hobbies. If I'm doing community theater, I'm fully committed. If I'm playing guitar, I'm practicing until my fingers bleed. But it never felt like... like this. It never felt like a runaway train. It was always directed, purposeful energy that I was in control of. I could turn it off, or at least turn it down, when I needed to sleep or, you know, just be a normal person having dinner with a friend.\n\nSo, no, this specific kind of... this terrifying, uncontrollable voltage, this absolute certainty that I was operating on a higher plane of existence while simultaneously wanting to bite the head off anyone who looked at me sideways... that's entirely new. That's what makes this whole month so incredibly disorienting. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual — a period that lasted for several days or more — even if it was before this recent month?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about whether I've ever had anything like this before, and I really, truly don't think I have... but if you're asking about this specific recent episode again, this past month... it's just so hard to articulate the exact texture of it. \n\nBecause, okay, at first... at first it didn't feel like a problem. It felt... well, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this profound, undeniable sensation that I had some urgent, critical purpose that no one else could possibly see or understand. Like I had tapped into some universal current that everyone else was just blindly ignoring. I'd be awake at 3 AM, completely wired, drafting these intricate, sweeping plans for the clinic, or sketching out these massive canvases, and I felt... I felt brilliant. I felt like I was finally operating at my true capacity. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is the part that really frightens me now, looking back—is how quickly that light turned into this scorching, toxic heat. If anyone, and I mean *anyone*, questioned what I was doing, or looked at me with even a hint of skepticism, or tried to slow me down... I would just snap. It wasn't just annoyance. It was this absolute, blinding rage. I remember my office manager, Sarah—who is a saint, truly—she just gently suggested that maybe I shouldn't be calling patients on a Sunday morning to discuss their treatment plans. And I absolutely tore into her. I said things that were so cruel, so wildly out of proportion, just because she was standing in the way of this... this momentum I felt I had. It was like I was a speeding car, and anyone who even stepped near the road was going to get obliterated. So, yeah, that elevated feeling, it was there, but it was so tightly coiled with this explosive hostility.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another episode in your life — before this recent one — where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Right, I’m sorry, I know I completely derailed your question just now and went off on that tangent about Sarah and the clinic. It’s just... my mind is still constantly picking through the wreckage of what I’ve done over the last few weeks, and it's hard to stay on track. But to give you a definitive, clear answer—no. I really, truly haven't. Before this past month, before losing Garrett, there was never a time in my life where I had an episode like that. \n\nAnd I feel like I can say that with a pretty high degree of certainty, especially given my line of work. I mean, being a psychologist, you spend your entire career trained to observe behavior, to track mood fluctuations, to look for these exact kinds of shifts in your clients. I’m hyper-aware of psychological baselines. If I had spent a week in my twenties or thirties sleeping two hours a night, feeling abnormally energized, or acting like I was operating on some elevated plane, I really believe I would have recognized it—or at the very least, one of my colleagues or supervisors would have pulled me aside and pointed it out. \n\nMy baseline for my entire adult life has been almost boringly stable. I’d wake up, go to the office, see my patients, come home... we’d cook dinner, maybe watch a movie, and I’d sleep a solid seven or eight hours. Every single night. I’ve always been very grounded, very predictable. I never had these wild swings or periods where I just didn't need sleep. That’s precisely why this whole experience has been so profoundly terrifying. It’s like a dormant volcano just erupted in the middle of my life and I didn't even know I was living on a fault line. So, looking back at everything before this recent crisis... no. There is absolutely zero history of it. This is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I know you're asking this again, and I really don't mean to be difficult or evasive. I know in a diagnostic setting, sometimes the clinician has to ask the same question in slightly different ways just to ensure nothing is being missed, to make sure the patient isn't minimizing something from their past. I do that with my own patients, so I understand the repetition. But I promise you, I have combed through my memories, I've looked at my twenties, my thirties, my time in undergrad, my time in my doctoral program, the early years of my marriage with Garrett... I've looked at all of it. And there is just... there is nothing there. 

I was always the steady one. Garrett used to joke that I was a metronome, just ticking away, keeping the tempo of our lives completely even. If I had a stressful week, I might be a little irritable, or maybe I'd need an extra cup of coffee in the morning, but I never—not once—had a week where I felt like I didn't need to sleep, or where I felt this bizarre, electric grandiosity. I never had a period where I was vibrating with so much energy that I couldn't sit still in my own skin. 

I've tried to think if maybe there was a time I just didn't notice it, you know? Like maybe I was just so busy building my practice that I mistook a manic... well, an elevated episode for just being highly motivated. But even then, I always crashed. I always needed sleep. I always had a very clear limit to my physical and mental endurance. I never had this feeling of being invincible, or this terrifying rage that came with it. It's just a complete blank. Before the funeral, before this last month... my life was just a straight, predictable line. There were no spikes. None at all.

It’s like the two things are inextricably linked, which, from a clinical perspective, is fascinating, but from a personal perspective, is utterly terrifying because I don't know how to turn either of them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually energetic, unusually confident, or needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, that ties exactly into what I was just saying about that period a few weeks ago, but to really get into the subjective phenomenology of it... it wasn't just that I had energy, it was the quality of that energy. At first, I felt almost lit up. That’s the only phrase that really does it justice. Lit up from the inside out, like my entire nervous system was suddenly conducting a much higher voltage. \n\nI felt this incredible, driving momentum, like I had some urgent purpose that no one else could see. I was completely convinced that I was on the precipice of some major breakthrough, whether it was with the cognitive behavioral book I was drafting in my head or the exact way the painting room needed to be physically structured to maximize my creative flow. I could see the whole picture perfectly, all the connections, the grand design of it all. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is where it became really problematic, practically speaking, and honestly quite out of character for me—was the irritability. Because when you feel that lit up, when you have that level of absolute clarity and purpose, any interference feels like a literal, physical threat. I’d snap into an absolute rage if anyone questioned me or tried to slow me down. \n\nMy sister, for instance, she came over unannounced because I hadn't been returning her texts for a few days, and she saw me in the middle of ripping up the floorboards, and she very gently suggested I might be overdoing it and should maybe try to get some sleep. And I just... I completely lost it on her. I screamed. I accused her of being small-minded, of being jealous, of actively trying to sabotage my life's work. It wasn't just your standard annoyance; it was this visceral, explosive anger that came out of nowhere just because she put a conversational speed bump in my way. So, yes, the confidence and the energy were astronomical, completely off the charts, but it was an incredibly volatile state to be in. The moment the outside world didn't perfectly align with my pace, I was just furious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of time following that elevated mood where you felt persistently down, hopeless, or lost interest in things that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, the crash. Because, you know, when you're running at that kind of voltage, it’s completely unsustainable. Eventually, the system just shorts out. And when it did, it wasn't just a gentle return to my normal baseline. It was like the floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nYou asked about feeling down or losing interest, and... well, \"losing interest\" almost sounds too mild, too passive for what this actually feels like. It's more like a total evaporation of meaning. The things that usually anchor me, the pillars of my day-to-day existence... they just feel entirely flat and unreachable now. Take my painting, for example. Just a short time ago, I was tearing up the floors to build the perfect studio, and now? I look at the canvases and I feel absolutely nothing. It’s just dead space. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art. Playing my guitar, which is usually my primary way to decompress and center myself... it's just sitting in the corner gathering dust. I can't even bring myself to pick it up, let alone tune it. Even rehearsing at the theater—I've been involved in this local community theater group for years, and it's usually such a vital, energizing outlet for me—but the very thought of going there and trying to project emotion when I'm completely hollow inside is just impossible. I can't do it. \n\nBut the most terrifying part, honestly, is the professional side of it. Sitting with my patients. I'm in the chair, I'm making the eye contact, I'm giving the appropriate empathetic responses, but there is this massive, impenetrable pane of glass between us. I can't connect to their struggles. It's like I’m just going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything. I wake up, I put on the clothes, I do the things I'm supposed to do, but the color is completely drained out of the entire world. It’s just this heavy, suffocating grayness, and I can't seem to find my way back to caring about any of the things that make me who I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if the period of elevated mood and energy you described lasted for at least seven consecutive days?\n\nPatient: Seven days... well, yes, definitely, but it’s... it’s so hard to just surgically extract that one specific week or so and look at it in isolation. Because the reality is that this whole... this entire crisis has been unfolding for about a month now, ever since Garrett died. \n\nIt’s actually staggering to think about it in terms of strict days or weeks, because time just becomes completely distorted when your brain is running at those extremes. You look at a calendar, and a month is just a flip of a page, right? Just four weeks. But for me, in this past month since losing him, I’ve had to navigate the initial, crushing grief, and then that massive, relentless surge of energy that just kept burning and burning day after day—absolutely for longer than seven consecutive days, it just wouldn't stop, it was a sustained state—and now, of course, this total, suffocating emptiness. \n\nIt feels like I've lived a decade squeezed into thirty days. So, yes, to answer your specific metric, the elevated part, that frantic overdrive, it didn't just flash and fade in a weekend. It sustained itself for well over a week. But clinically and personally, it's all just one piece of this massive month-long continuum that I've been trapped in since his passing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any other periods in your life where you felt similarly unusually energetic, confident, or required much less sleep than usual—periods that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods where I was productive, sure. You know, you go through grad school, you push through a dissertation, you open a private practice—you have to have a certain amount of drive to accomplish those things. There are weeks where you're just firing on all cylinders, where you're staying up late reading research papers or writing case notes, and you feel good about it. You feel capable. But that's just... that's just being an ambitious professional, isn't it? \n\nI've always been someone who throws himself into his work and his hobbies. If I'm doing community theater, I'm fully committed. If I'm playing guitar, I'm practicing until my fingers bleed. But it never felt like... like this. It never felt like a runaway train. It was always directed, purposeful energy that I was in control of. I could turn it off, or at least turn it down, when I needed to sleep or, you know, just be a normal person having dinner with a friend.\n\nSo, no, this specific kind of... this terrifying, uncontrollable voltage, this absolute certainty that I was operating on a higher plane of existence while simultaneously wanting to bite the head off anyone who looked at me sideways... that's entirely new. That's what makes this whole month so incredibly disorienting. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual — a period that lasted for several days or more — even if it was before this recent month?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about whether I've ever had anything like this before, and I really, truly don't think I have... but if you're asking about this specific recent episode again, this past month... it's just so hard to articulate the exact texture of it. \n\nBecause, okay, at first... at first it didn't feel like a problem. It felt... well, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this profound, undeniable sensation that I had some urgent, critical purpose that no one else could possibly see or understand. Like I had tapped into some universal current that everyone else was just blindly ignoring. I'd be awake at 3 AM, completely wired, drafting these intricate, sweeping plans for the clinic, or sketching out these massive canvases, and I felt... I felt brilliant. I felt like I was finally operating at my true capacity. \n\nBut the flip side of that—and this is the part that really frightens me now, looking back—is how quickly that light turned into this scorching, toxic heat. If anyone, and I mean *anyone*, questioned what I was doing, or looked at me with even a hint of skepticism, or tried to slow me down... I would just snap. It wasn't just annoyance. It was this absolute, blinding rage. I remember my office manager, Sarah—who is a saint, truly—she just gently suggested that maybe I shouldn't be calling patients on a Sunday morning to discuss their treatment plans. And I absolutely tore into her. I said things that were so cruel, so wildly out of proportion, just because she was standing in the way of this... this momentum I felt I had. It was like I was a speeding car, and anyone who even stepped near the road was going to get obliterated. So, yeah, that elevated feeling, it was there, but it was so tightly coiled with this explosive hostility.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another episode in your life — before this recent one — where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Right, I’m sorry, I know I completely derailed your question just now and went off on that tangent about Sarah and the clinic. It’s just... my mind is still constantly picking through the wreckage of what I’ve done over the last few weeks, and it's hard to stay on track. But to give you a definitive, clear answer—no. I really, truly haven't. Before this past month, before losing Garrett, there was never a time in my life where I had an episode like that. \n\nAnd I feel like I can say that with a pretty high degree of certainty, especially given my line of work. I mean, being a psychologist, you spend your entire career trained to observe behavior, to track mood fluctuations, to look for these exact kinds of shifts in your clients. I’m hyper-aware of psychological baselines. If I had spent a week in my twenties or thirties sleeping two hours a night, feeling abnormally energized, or acting like I was operating on some elevated plane, I really believe I would have recognized it—or at the very least, one of my colleagues or supervisors would have pulled me aside and pointed it out. \n\nMy baseline for my entire adult life has been almost boringly stable. I’d wake up, go to the office, see my patients, come home... we’d cook dinner, maybe watch a movie, and I’d sleep a solid seven or eight hours. Every single night. I’ve always been very grounded, very predictable. I never had these wild swings or periods where I just didn't need sleep. That’s precisely why this whole experience has been so profoundly terrifying. It’s like a dormant volcano just erupted in the middle of my life and I didn't even know I was living on a fault line. So, looking back at everything before this recent crisis... no. There is absolutely zero history of it. This is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life — even just one or two — where your mood was elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual, lasting for several days or more, outside of this recent month?\n\nPatient: I... I know you're asking this again, and I really don't mean to be difficult or evasive. I know in a diagnostic setting, sometimes the clinician has to ask the same question in slightly different ways just to ensure nothing is being missed, to make sure the patient isn't minimizing something from their past. I do that with my own patients, so I understand the repetition. But I promise you, I have combed through my memories, I've looked at my twenties, my thirties, my time in undergrad, my time in my doctoral program, the early years of my marriage with Garrett... I've looked at all of it. And there is just... there is nothing there. \n\nI was always the steady one. Garrett used to joke that I was a metronome, just ticking away, keeping the tempo of our lives completely even. If I had a stressful week, I might be a little irritable, or maybe I'd need an extra cup of coffee in the morning, but I never—not once—had a week where I felt like I didn't need to sleep, or where I felt this bizarre, electric grandiosity. I never had a period where I was vibrating with so much energy that I couldn't sit still in my own skin. \n\nI've tried to think if maybe there was a time I just didn't notice it, you know? Like maybe I was just so busy building my practice that I mistook a manic... well, an elevated episode for just being highly motivated. But even then, I always crashed. I always needed sleep. I always had a very clear limit to my physical and mental endurance. I never had this feeling of being invincible, or this terrifying rage that came with it. It's just a complete blank. Before the funeral, before this last month... my life was just a straight, predictable line. There were no spikes. None at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That’s precisely why this whole experience has been so profoundly terrifying. It’s like a dormant volcano just erupted in the middle of my life and I didn't even know I was living on a fault line. So, looking back at everything before this recent crisis... no. There is absolutely zero history of it. This is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life — even just one or two — where your mood was elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual, lasting for several days or more, outside of this recent month?\n\nPatient: I... I know in a diagnostic setting, sometimes the clinician has to ask the same question in slightly different ways just to ensure nothing is being missed, to make sure the patient isn't minimizing something from their past. I do that with my own patients, so I understand the repetition. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we are going in circles here, doctor, and I’m sorry, I really am. I know you’re doing your due diligence by asking me this again, trying to shake loose some forgotten memory from ten or twenty years ago. You're looking for a historical pattern, and I understand why. But I keep coming back to this recent episode because it is literally the only time I have ever felt anything even remotely like this, and frankly, it is consuming my every waking thought right now. 

I really need you to understand the sheer magnitude of what happened to me this past month, because it wasn't just me being "energetic" or needing less sleep. It was a complete hijacking of my reality. Like I was trying to explain earlier... at first, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this intoxicating, brilliant feeling, like I was suddenly infused with this urgent, profound purpose that absolutely no one else around me could see or comprehend. I felt like I was operating on this higher frequency, seeing the hidden machinery of the world while everyone else was just sleepwalking through their mundane little lives. 

But the terrifying part, the part I just can't reconcile or forgive myself for, is how quickly that light mutated. Because I had that urgent purpose, if anyone questioned me—if anyone even hesitated, or looked confused, or tried to slow me down or get in my way—I would just snap into this uncontrollable rage. It wasn't just normal irritability or annoyance. It was a visceral, destructive fury because they were impeding this grand, critical mission I thought I was on. I was so incredibly cruel to people who were just trying to help me. 

So, to answer your question again... no. Outside of this past month, there is nothing. If I had ever experienced that kind of blinding, euphoric light and that kind of monstrous, hair-trigger anger before, I promise you, I would have known. It's not something you can just forget or sleep off.

It would have ruined my schooling, it would have terrified Garrett... it would have been a massive, defining trauma that I would have spent years in therapy trying to unpack. But it wasn't there. My life was quiet. It was stable and incredibly normal. I was just a regular person living a regular life until this past month when everything just shattered. It's like I was suddenly hijacked by a completely different nervous system, and looking back at my entire forty-one years of life, I can't find a single precedent for it. It's just this one, massive, horrifying anomaly that started after Garrett... well, after the funeral. Before that, I was just a regular guy who occasionally worked too hard. Never this. Never, ever this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode in your life where your mood was abnormally elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual — a period that lasted for several days or more — even if it was before this recent month?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about whether I've ever had anything like this before, and I really, truly don't think I have... but if you're asking about this specific recent episode again, this past month... it's just so hard to articulate the exact texture of it. \n\nBecause, okay, at first... at first it didn't feel like a problem. It felt... well, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this profound, undeniable sensation that I had some urgent, critical purpose that no one else could possibly see or understand. It was this absolute, blinding rage. This is the very first time anything like this has ever happened to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life — even just one or two — where your mood was elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual, lasting for several days or more, outside of this recent month?\n\nPatient: I... I know you're asking this again, and I really don't mean to be difficult or evasive. I know in a diagnostic setting, sometimes the clinician has to ask the same question in slightly different ways just to ensure nothing is being missed, to make sure the patient isn't minimizing something from their past. I do that with my own patients, so I understand the repetition. But I promise you, I have combed through my memories, I've looked at my twenties, my thirties, my time in undergrad, my time in my doctoral program, the early years of my marriage with Garrett... I've looked at all of it. And there is just... there is nothing there. \n\nI was always the steady one. Garrett used to joke that I was a metronome, just ticking away, keeping the tempo of our lives completely even. If I had a stressful week, I might be a little irritable, or maybe I'd need an extra cup of coffee in the morning, but I never—not once—had a week where I felt like I didn't need to sleep, or where I felt this bizarre, electric grandiosity. I never had a period where I was vibrating with so much energy that I couldn't sit still in my own skin. \n\nI've tried to think if maybe there was a time I just didn't notice it, you know? Like maybe I was just so busy building my practice that I mistook a manic... well, an elevated episode for just being highly motivated. But even then, I always crashed. I always needed sleep. I always had a very clear limit to my physical and mental endurance. I never had this feeling of being invincible, or this terrifying rage that came with it. It's just a complete blank. Before the funeral, before this last month... my life was just a straight, predictable line. There were no spikes. None at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other distinct periods in your life — even just one or two — where your mood was elevated, you felt unusually energetic, or you needed much less sleep than usual, lasting for several days or more, outside of this recent month?\n\nPatient: I know we are going in circles here, doctor, and I’m sorry, I really am. I know you’re doing your due diligence by asking me this again, trying to shake loose some forgotten memory from ten or twenty years ago. You're looking for a historical pattern, and I understand why. But I keep coming back to this recent episode because it is literally the only time I have ever felt anything even remotely like this, and frankly, it is consuming my every waking thought right now. \n\nI really need you to understand the sheer magnitude of what happened to me this past month, because it wasn't just me being \"energetic\" or needing less sleep. It was a complete hijacking of my reality. Like I was trying to explain earlier... at first, I felt almost lit up from the inside. It was this intoxicating, brilliant feeling, like I was suddenly infused with this urgent, profound purpose that absolutely no one else around me could see or comprehend. I felt like I was operating on this higher frequency, seeing the hidden machinery of the world while everyone else was just sleepwalking through their mundane little lives. \n\nBut the terrifying part, the part I just can't reconcile or forgive myself for, is how quickly that light mutated. Because I had that urgent purpose, if anyone questioned me—if anyone even hesitated, or looked confused, or tried to slow me down or get in my way—I would just snap into this uncontrollable rage. It wasn't just normal irritability or annoyance. It was a visceral, destructive fury because they were impeding this grand, critical mission I thought I was on. I was so incredibly cruel to people who were just trying to help me. \n\nSo, to answer your question again... no. Outside of this past month, there is nothing. If I had ever experienced that kind of blinding, euphoric light and that kind of monstrous, hair-trigger anger before, I promise you, I would have known. It's not something you can just forget or sleep off.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of at least one other time in your life — not counting this past month — when you had a distinct period of elevated mood, unusual energy, or needed much less sleep than usual that lasted for several days or more?\n\nPatient: I... I honestly don't know how many different ways I can say it, doctor. I know you're doing your job, I really do, but I feel like I'm on the witness stand trying to prove an alibi for a life I didn't lead. I have searched my entire history for you. I have mentally walked through my childhood, my twenties, my thirties... every single year up to being forty-one right now. And the answer is just no. None. Zero. There is not a single other time in my entire existence where my brain did... whatever this was. \n\nYou have to understand, I wouldn't just forget something like this. What happened this past month wasn't just having a little extra energy to clean the house or staying up late to finish clinical notes. It was a complete and total fracture of my reality. I thought I was receiving direct, coded instructions from the television. I thought I had uncovered this massive, cosmic secret that made sleep completely irrelevant, like my body had somehow evolved past the biological need for rest. It was terrifying, and beautiful, and completely destructive all at once. \n\nIf I had ever experienced even a fraction of that before—if I had ever felt that terrifying, god-like energy or that vicious anger that came when people didn't understand my 'mission'—it would have derailed my life back then. It would have ruined my schooling, it would have terrified Garrett... it would have been a massive, defining trauma that I would have spent years in therapy trying to unpack. But it wasn't there. My life was quiet. It was stable and incredibly normal. I was just a regular person living a regular life until this past month when everything just shattered. So, please, you have to believe me when I tell you... this is the only time. There is nothing else in my past to find."
